7.3    Solutions attached to the branch factors
In this section, we prove the second statement of Theorem 6.2. The proof reduces the conjugated system
(7.21)–(7.22) to a one-dimensional problem along a carefully chosen shifted ray, constructed in Proposi-
tion 7.4. In Proposition 7.5, we show that along this ray the scaled square-free derivatives satisfy a block
first-order system in the radial variable λ. The non-empty derivative block has a positive spectral gap, which
allows us to impose the condition that this block decays at infinity by solving a Volterra integral equation.
The Volterra operator is a contraction, and hence gives a unique solution of the first-order system with the
desired asymptotic estimates. Translating these estimates back from the scaled derivative system gives the
desired estimates for W and its square-free derivatives.
Proposition 7.4. Let C be the domain (6.7). Fix
                                             σ = (σ1 , . . . , σn+m ) ∈ {±1}n+m ,
and define
                           ω = (ω1 , . . . , ωn+m ),       ωj = j + iσj ,        1 ⩽ j ⩽ n + m.
Then, for every
                                                  z = (z1 , . . . , zn+m ) ∈ C
and every r ⩾ 0, we have z + rω ∈ C and
                                                        1        
                                               R z + rω ⩾ R(z) + r ,                                    (7.38)
                                                         4
where R(z) is defined in (6.6).


                                                                83
Proof. For 1 ⩽ ℓ < n + m, we have
                                               
                       Re zℓ+1 + rωℓ+1 − zℓ + rωℓ = Re[zℓ+1 − zℓ ] + r > D.

Thus z + rω ∈ C.
  Fix 1 ⩽ i < j ⩽ n + m. Since j − i ⩾ 1,
                                            
                          zj + rωj − zi + rωi ⩾ Re[zj − zi ] + r(j − i) ⩾ r.

On the other hand,
                          
        zj + rωj − zi + rωi = |zj − zi + r(j − i) + ir(σj − σi )| ⩾ |zj − zi + r(j − i)| − r|σj − σi |.

Since Re[zj −zi ] > 0 and r(j −i) ⩾ 0, adding r(j −i) to the real part cannot decrease the modulus. Moreover,
|σj − σi | ⩽ 2. Therefore
                                                  
                              zj + rωj − zi + rωi ⩾ |zj − zi | − 2r ⩾ R(z) − 2r.

Combining the two lower bounds gives
                                                     3   1          1        
             zj + rωj − zi + rωi ⩾ max {r, R(z) − 2r} ⩾ r + R(z) − 2r = R(z) + r .
                                                       4   4           4
Taking the minimum over 1 ⩽ i < j ⩽ n + m proves (7.38).

Proposition 7.5 (Radial block system along shifted rays). Let C be the domain (6.7), fix a sign pattern

                     (ϵ, η) = σ = (ϵ1 , . . . , ϵn , η1 , . . . , ηm ) = (σ1 , . . . , σn+m ) ∈ {±1}n+m ,

and let
                                       z = (t, s) ∈ C,         R0 ⩽ R(z) ⩽ 2R0 .
Let ω be as in Proposition 7.4, and define

                                   γz,ω (λ) := z + (λ − R0 )ω,             λ ∈ [R0 , ∞).

   Let J be the set of square-free index pairs

                                  (I, A),       I ⊆ {1, . . . , n},    A ⊆ {1, . . . , m},

and write
                                                  J ∗ := J \ {(∅, ∅)}.
Order the components indexed by J ∗ by increasing |I| + |A|.
   Let W be a holomorphic solution of the conjugated bulk system (7.21)–(7.22). Define

                        YI,A (λ; z) := λ|I|+|A| ∂tI ∂sA W γz,ω (λ) ,
                                                                  
                                                                     (I, A) ∈ J ,

and write
                                                                                  
                                    Y = (Y0 , Y∗ ),        Y0 (λ; z) := W γz,ω (λ) ,
where Y∗ collects the components corresponding to (I, A) ∈ J ∗ .
   Then there exist matrix-valued functions

                                      C01 (λ; z),       C10 (λ; z),        C11 (λ; z),


                                                             84
and a matrix Λ(λ; z) on the non-empty derivative block, whose entries are uniformly bounded for λ ⩾ R0 ,
such that
                                d      1

                               dλ      λ
                                d                 1              1

                               dλ                 λ              λ
The matrix Λ(λ; z) is block lower triangular with respect to the ordering by |I| + |A|. More precisely, for
(I, A), (J, B) ∈ J ∗ with (I, A) ̸= (J, B),

                            Λ(I,A),(J,B) (λ; z) = 0       unless       |J| + |B| < |I| + |A|.                          (7.41)

Its diagonal entries are independent of λ and z, and are given by
                                                               X            X
                         Λ(I,A),(I,A) (λ; z) = ΛI,A (ω) := −2i   ϵi ωi − βi   ηa ωn+a .                                (7.42)
                                                                     i∈I              a∈A

In particular,
                                    Re ΛI,A (ω) = 2|I| + β|A|,             (I, A) ∈ J ∗ .                              (7.43)

   Next suppose i ∈ I. Write I ′ = I \ {i}. Then the relevant derivative is

                                                                       (t)

                                                                                          (t)

                                                                                              


Multiplying by ωi , the leading term in (7.48) contributes

                                                    −2iϵi ωi YI,A .                                                 (7.49)

   We now estimate the second term on the right-hand side of (7.48). The coefficients

                                           1              1                1
                                                ,              ,                                                    (7.50)
                                        ti − tj        sa − sb          ti − sa

have degree −1. From (7.19), the functions Ωi and Σa also have degree −1. Hence, in (7.24) and (7.26), the
coefficients of first-order derivatives have degree −1. Finally, the formulas (7.27) and (7.28) show directly
        (t)        (s)
that Vi and Va are sums of terms of degree −2. Therefore, along the shifted ray, for square-free pairs
(J, B),
                                                                            (t)
  ∂tJ ∂sB a first-order coefficient = O λ−1−|J|−|B| ,                             ∂tJ ∂sB Va(s) = O λ−2−|J|−|B| .
                                                                                                             
                                                               ∂tJ ∂sB Vi ,                                         (7.51)

Here we used Proposition 7.4, which gives R(γz,ω (λ)) ⩾ λ/4.
                                                                  (t)
    Consider first a term obtained from a first-order part of Ri . Suppose that, after applying Leibniz’ rule,
exactly r of the derivatives in ∂tI ′ ∂sA fall on its coefficient. The coefficient is then O(λ−1−r ), while the
differential monomial acting on W has total order |I| + |A| − r. If this monomial is square-free, then, after
passing to the scaled variables, its coefficient is

                                        λ|I|+|A| λ−1−r λ−(|I|+|A|−r) = λ−1 .

Hence it belongs to the λ−1 C11 Y∗ part of (7.40).
   If the resulting monomial is not square-free, it contains exactly one repeated variable. Suppose first that
the repeated variable is tj . We use
                                                                        (t)
                                          ∂t2j W = −2iϵj ∂tj W − Rj W.

The principal term −2iϵj ∂tj W removes the repetition without supplying an additional factor λ−1 . The
resulting square-free derivative has total order |I| + |A| − r − 1, and its scaled coefficient is

                                        λ|I|+|A| λ−1−r λ−(|I|+|A|−r−1) = 1.


                                                          86
Since
                                          |I| + |A| − r − 1 < |I| + |A|,
this gives a uniformly bounded coupling from a strictly lower-order square-free derivative into the equation
for YI,A . The case in which the repeated variable is sb is identical, using (7.47).
                                                        (t)          (s)
    If instead we use the corresponding remainder Rj W , or Rb W when the repeated variable is sb , every
first-order coefficient supplies one additional factor λ−1 , while the total order of the derivative on W decreases
by one. Derivatives falling on that new coefficient increase its decay and decrease the remaining derivative
order by the same amount. A scalar coefficient supplies two powers of λ−1 , and the resulting differential
monomial is square-free. Thus the sum

                      decay order of the coefficient + total order of the derivative on W

remains equal to |I| + |A| + 1. The new differential monomial is either square-free, in which case its scaled
coefficient is O(λ−1 ), or it contains again exactly one repeated variable. In the latter case we repeat the
same reduction. Each use of a remainder decreases the total order of the derivative on W , so the procedure
terminates after finitely many steps.
    There are only two possible terminal cases. If no principal term is used at the last step, the final coefficient
decay order and the final square-free derivative order add up to |I| + |A| + 1, and hence the scaled coefficient
is O(λ−1 ). If a principal term is used, it removes the unique repetition and lowers the derivative order by
one without changing the coefficient decay. The final coefficient decay order and the final derivative order
then add up to |I| + |A|; the scaled coefficient is O(1), and the final square-free derivative has strictly smaller
total order than |I| + |A|.
                                                          (t)
    A term obtained directly from the scalar part Vi is simpler. If exactly r derivatives in ∂tI ′ ∂sA fall
on the scalar coefficient, then the coefficient is O(λ−2−r ), while the derivative acting on W has total order
|I| + |A| − 1 − r. Hence its scaled coefficient is

                                      λ|I|+|A| λ−2−r λ−(|I|+|A|−1−r) = λ−1 .

Thus all scalar terms contribute to the λ−1 C10 Y0 or λ−1 C11 Y∗ part of (7.40).
  Similarly, if a ∈ A and
                                                A′ = A \ {a},
then
                                                 λ|I|+|A| ∂s2a ∂tI ∂sA′ W
is reduced using (7.47). Its leading contribution, before multiplication by ωn+a , is

                                             −iβηa λ|I|+|A| ∂tI ∂sA W.

After multiplying by ωn+a , this contributes

                                                   −βiηa ωn+a YI,A .                                         (7.52)
                                           (s)
Again, all remaining terms come from Ra , and the same conclusion as above holds.
                                                                                (t)   (s)
   We have therefore proved the following dichotomy for every term produced by Ri or Ra :

   • it is a uniformly bounded multiple of a square-free derivative of strictly smaller total order; or

   • it carries a factor λ−1 , after which its coefficient is uniformly bounded.

The first class gives the strict block-lower-triangular part of Λ(λ; z), while the second class gives C10 and
C11 . Notice also that an O(1) term cannot involve Y0 , because a principal reduction always leaves at least
one derivative of W .


                                                           87
   Combining the leading contributions (7.49) and (7.52) for all i ∈ I and a ∈ A, the diagonal coefficient of
YI,A is
                                                X             X
                                ΛI,A (ω) := −2i    ϵi ωi − βi     ηa ωn+a .
                                                       i∈I            a∈A

This gives (7.40), (7.41), and (7.42). Uniform boundedness follows from (7.51), the fact that there are only
finitely many square-free derivatives, and the uniform boundedness of the components of ω.
   Step 3: The real parts of the diagonal entries.
   It remains to compute the real part of the diagonal entries. By the construction of ω in (7.4),

                      Im[ωi ] = ϵi ,      1 ⩽ i ⩽ n,          Im[ωn+a ] = ηa ,       1 ⩽ a ⩽ m.

Hence
                                          Re[−2iϵi ωi ] = 2ϵi Im[ωi ] = 2,
and
                                       Re[−βiηa ωn+a ] = βηa Im[ωn+a ] = β.
Therefore Re[ΛI,A (ω)] = 2|I| + β|A|, which proves (7.43).

Proof of the second statement in Theorem 6.2. Fix first z = (t, s) ∈ C, set R0 := R(z), let ω be as in (7.4),
and consider the shifted ray

                                 γz,ω (λ) := z + (λ − R0 )ω,           λ ∈ [R0 , ∞).

                                              c0 := min{2, β} > 0.

Step 4: Existence, asymptotics, and raywise uniqueness.
    If R0 is sufficiently large, then C1 R0−1 < 1, so by (7.65) T is a contraction on Bz . Hence (7.61) admits
a unique fixed point Y∗ ∈ Bz . Defining Y0 by (7.60), we obtain a unique solution (Y0 , Y∗ ) of (7.53)–(7.54)
satisfying
                                     Y0 (λ) → 1,     Y∗ (λ) → 0,     λ → ∞.
Moreover, (7.63) and (7.64) imply

                            Y0 (λ) = 1 + O(λ−1 ),             Y∗ (λ) = O(λ−1 ),           λ ⩾ R0 .

   Now define
                                                     W (z) := Y0 (R0 ).
By construction,
                                      W (z) = 1 + O(R0−1 ) = 1 + O(R(z)−1 ).
For each non-empty square-free pair (I, A) ∈ J ∗ , the corresponding component of Y∗ (R0 ) is

                                        YI,A (R0 ) = R(z)|I|+|A| ∂tI ∂sA W (z).

Thus the bound Y∗ (R0 ) = O(R0−1 ) gives

                                           ∂tI ∂sA W (z) = O R(z)−|I|−|A|−1 .
                                                                           


                                                               90
   The same argument applied to the difference of two normalized solutions has no constant term in (7.60).
Therefore the corresponding Volterra map is a strict contraction with zero forcing, and the difference vanishes.
This proves uniqueness along every admissible shifted ray.

Step 5: Holomorphic dependence and gluing on the ordered domain.
    Fix z0 ∈ C. After shrinking to a small neighborhood U of z0 , we may choose a fixed number R0 such that
R0 ⩽ R(z) ⩽ 2R0 for all z ∈ U. Use the same vector ω and the rays z + (λ − R0 )ω, λ ⩾ R0 , throughout
U. Proposition 7.4 gives the required separation uniformly on these rays. Since R0 and ω are fixed, the
coefficients of the Volterra equation depend holomorphically on z, and the contraction estimate is uniform.
Therefore its fixed point depends holomorphically on z.
    We have so far solved the first-order system only in the radial direction. To check the remaining co-
ordinate directions, fix one coordinate and compare two ways of differentiating the constructed square-free
jet: differentiation with respect to the base point, and the corresponding coordinate equation of the full
square-free first-order system. Their difference is the error in that coordinate equation. By flatness, ra-
dial differentiation commutes with every coordinate equation, so this error satisfies the homogeneous radial
system. The estimates above hold uniformly for nearby base points, and therefore the error has zero nor-
malization at infinity. Raywise uniqueness forces it to vanish. Thus the constructed jet is a holomorphic
                                                                                                           (U)
horizontal section of the full flat first-order system, and its empty component is a holomorphic solution Wϵ,η
of the conjugated system.
    The local constructions agree on overlaps. Indeed, all neighborhoods use the same vector ω. At a point
z in an overlap, restrict both horizontal sections to the geometric ray z + rω, r ⩾ 0. After using the same
radial parameter, their scaled square-free jets solve the same radial system and have the same normalization
at infinity. The raywise uniqueness from Step 4 therefore shows that their values at r = 0 agree. Hence the
local solutions glue to a holomorphic function Wϵ,η on all of C, and the estimates (6.9)–(6.10) hold there
with constants depending only on β, n, m. Finally, Fϵ,η := Φϵ,η Wϵ,η solves the original system.

Step 6: Linear independence.
   From the explicit formula for Φϵ,η , we have

                                                                                          β
                        ∂ti log Φϵ,η = iϵi + O(D−1 ),                     ∂sa log Φϵ,η = i ηa + O(D−1 ).
                                                                                          2

For a square-free pair (I, A), the explicit formula for the branch factor and the estimates for Wϵ,η give,
uniformly on C,
                                                                    |A| Y Y
                                                                    β
                           Φ−1
                            ϵ,η ∂tI ∂sA Fϵ,η = i
                                                |I|+|A|
                                                                           ϵi ηa + O(D−1 ).                  (7.66)
                                                                    2
                                                                                 i∈I   a∈A


After dividing each row by its nonzero prefactor, the leading matrix is the character table
                                                                             !
                                                       Y          Y
                                                             ϵi         ηa
                                                       i∈I        a∈A         (I,A),(ϵ,η)


of {±1}n+m . In particular, its rows satisfy the orthogonality relations
                                                       !                        
                          X Y               Y                Y          Y

                           ϵ,η   i∈I        a∈A              j∈J        b∈B


and therefore the leading matrix is invertible. Increasing D, if necessary, the matrix in (7.66) is also invertible.
Therefore the 2n+m solutions Fϵ,η are linearly independent.

8     Solutions of the edge deformed CMS operators
The proof of Theorem 6.5 is parallel to the bulk case. We prove the first statement of Theorem 6.5, namely
that the solution space has dimension 2n+m , in Section 8.1. We derive the conjugated equations in Section 8.2.
We then prove the second statement, namely the construction of solutions with prescribed leading behavior,
in Section 8.3.

Proof. We begin with the zeroth-order estimates. We use the elementary inequality

                             |u(u ± v)| ≳ min |u|2 , |u2 − v 2 | ,
                                                                
                                                                   u, v ∈ C.                        (8.36)

Indeed, if |v| ⩾ 2|u|, then
                                               |u ± v| ⩾ |v| − |u| ⩾ |u|,
so the left-hand side of (8.36) is at least |u|2 . If |v| ⩽ 2|u|, then

                                              |u ∓ v| ⩽ |u| + |v| ⩽ 3|u|,

and therefore
                                      |u2 − v 2 | = |u + v| |u − v| ⩽ 3|u| |u ± v|.
This proves (8.36).
   Applying (8.36) to the terms in (8.11), we obtain, for j ̸= i,
                              √      √       p                         

and                                       √

Thus every complete summand in (8.11) is O(R−1 ). Since the numbers of summands depend only on n and

uniformly in the sectors under consideration. Since |ti | ⩾ R, |sa | ⩾ R, and R is sufficiently large, this proves
(8.32).
    We next establish the derivative estimates. The angular restrictions in (6.41) place every coordinate in
a sector having a fixed positive angular distance from the real axis and from the branch cut of the principal
square root. Consequently, there exists a sufficiently small constant c > 0 such that the polydisc obtained by
moving each coordinate by at most 4cR remains in the same half-plane and in a fixed slightly larger sector
on which the square-root branches and the Airy asymptotics are valid.
    After decreasing c if necessary, every coordinate modulus and every pairwise difference on this polydisc
remain bounded below by a constant multiple of R. Indeed, the definition of R gives

                                  |ti |, |sa |, |ti − tj |, |ti − sa |, |sa − sb | ⩾ R

at the center, while moving each coordinate by at most 4cR changes a coordinate modulus by at most 4cR
and a pairwise difference by at most 8cR.
    It follows that the zeroth-order estimates proved above hold uniformly throughout this polydisc. In
particular,
                                               √             β√

there. These functions are holomorphic on the polydisc. Applying the multivariable Cauchy estimates on

   Finally, after collecting the coefficient of each first-order derivative in (8.15) and (8.17), every such

multiplied by constants depending only on β. Each complete coefficient is therefore O(R−1 ) on the same
polydisc and is holomorphic there. Cauchy’s estimates give the asserted O(R−1−ℓ ) bound for all of its
derivatives.

8.3    Solutions attached to the branch factors
Proposition 8.4. Let C = C(τ , D) be the domain (6.41), and let

                                      σ = (σ1 , . . . , σn+m ) ∈ {±1}n+m .

There exist a vector ω = ω(τ , σ), independent of the base point z ∈ C, and a constant c > 0, depending only
on n + m, such that, for every z ∈ C and every r ⩾ 0, we have z + rω ∈ C and
                                                
                       R(z + rω) ⩾ c R(z) + r ,                                                       (8.37)
                                    p              23π
                        arg −σj ωj zj + rωj ⩽           ,     1 ⩽ j ⩽ n + m,                          (8.38)
                                                     48
                                  p                       1/2

Here the square root is the principal square root.

Proposition 8.5 (Radial block system for the edge problem). Fix a sign pattern

                                            (ϵ, η) ∈ {±1}n × {±1}m .

Let C be a domain of the form (6.41), fix

                                     z = (t, s) ∈ C,          R0 ⩽ R(z) ⩽ 2R0 ,

and let ω be the fixed vector provided by Proposition 8.4 for

                                         σ := (ϵ1 , . . . , ϵn , η1 , . . . , ηm ).

Define
                                γz,ω (λ) := z + (λ − R0 )ω,                 λ ∈ [R0 , ∞).
    Let W be a holomorphic solution of the conjugated edge system (8.12)–(8.13). Let J be the set of square-
free index pairs
                              (I, A),      I ⊆ {1, . . . , n}, A ⊆ {1, . . . , m},
and write
                                              J ∗ := J \ {(∅, ∅)}.


                                                            99
For (I, A) ∈ J , define the scaled square-free derivatives along γz,ω by

                                      UI,A (λ; z, ω) := λ|I|+|A| ∂tI ∂sA W γz,ω (λ) .
                                                                                   


Write                                                                                   

where U∗ collects the components indexed by J ∗ .

on the non-empty derivative block. The entries of B01 , B10 , B11 are uniformly bounded for ζ ⩾ ζ0 , and the

    Order the components of U∗ by increasing total derivative order |I| + |A|. Then Γ(ζ; z, ω) is block lower

                                                               102
Thus this is a principal off-diagonal contribution from a strictly lower square-free derivative order. After
the change of variable ζ = 2λ3/2 /3, its coefficient acquires the factor λ−1/2 . Such a coefficient need not
be uniformly bounded when some coordinates are much larger than the separation scale; it will instead be
controlled by the relative estimate below.
    If we use rj rather than the leading square-root part of qj , then rj = O(λ−1 ), and the resulting term
                                          (t)
belongs to the λ−1 -part. If we use −Rj W , the reduction may continue. At this stage the remaining
square-free derivatives no longer contain tj . Consequently, the term Ωj ∂tj W , as well as the ∂tj -part of a
pair-interaction operator, cannot create another repeated derivative and therefore terminates the reduction
with a λ−1 -term. A scalar term also terminates the reduction and contributes only to the λ−1 -part. The
only terms that can continue a principal reduction are pair-interaction terms whose first-order derivative
has an index still present among the remaining square-free derivatives. Such a term creates a new repeated
derivative, to which the corresponding reduction rule is applied.
    The same discussion applies when the first-order summand involves ∂sb W . In particular, a principal
reduction chain may pass between the t- and s-variables, but at every step the next repeated index is
already present among the remaining square-free derivatives. Thus no new index can occur in a principal
off-diagonal term. If a principal contribution from UJ,B appears in the equation for UI,A , then

                                  J ⊆ I,         B ⊆ A,           |J| + |B| < |I| + |A|.

This proves the strict block-lower-triangular structure of the principal matrix.
   We next prove the relative estimate for the principal off-diagonal entries. It is enough to consider a chain
beginning with the radial ti -derivative; the argument for a chain beginning with an sa -derivative is identical.

where the entries of C01 , C10 , C11 are uniformly bounded. Under the ordering by increasing |I| + |A|, the

Proof of the second statement in Theorem 6.5. Fix a sign pattern (ϵ, η), and let ω be the fixed vector from

                                                         105
   For ξ ⩾ ζ, let E(ζ, ξ; z, ω) be the backward evolution generated by the full triangular matrix Γ, namely

    We estimate the terms in this finite expansion. Consider a strictly increasing chain of non-empty square-

Step 4: Existence, asymptotics, and raywise uniqueness.
    If R0 , equivalently ζ0 , is sufficiently large, then C2 ζ0−1 < 1, so by (8.66) T is a contraction on Bz . Hence
(8.62) admits a unique fixed point U∗ ∈ Bz . Defining U0 by (8.61), we obtain a unique solution (U0 , U∗ ) of
(8.56)–(8.57) satisfying
                                        U0 (ζ) → 1,      U∗ (ζ) → 0,      ζ → ∞.
Moreover, (8.64) and (8.65) imply

                             U0 (ζ) = 1 + O(ζ −1 ),           U∗ (ζ) = O(ζ −1 ),      ζ ⩾ ζ0 .                        (8.67)

   Now define
                                                       W (z) := U0 (ζ0 ).
             3/2
Since ζ0 = 2R0 /3, we have
                                      W (z) = 1 + O(ζ0−1 ) = 1 + O R(z)−3/2 .
                                                                           

For every (I, A) ∈ J ∗ , (8.67) also gives

                                                  UI,A (ζ0 ) = O R(z)−3/2 .
                                                                         

At this stage these are estimates for the components of the constructed square-free jet. In Step 5 we verify
that this jet is horizontal for the full flat system; its components are then identified with the square-free
derivatives of W , yielding the desired derivative estimates.
   The same argument applied to the difference of two normalized solutions has no constant term in (8.61).
Therefore the corresponding Volterra map is a strict contraction with zero forcing, and the difference vanishes.
This proves uniqueness along every admissible shifted ray.
Step 5: Holomorphic dependence and gluing on C.
    Fix z0 ∈ C. Choose a fixed number R0 > 0, and shrink to a sufficiently small neighborhood U ⊂ C of z0 ,
so that
                                      R0 ⩽ R(z) ⩽ 2R0 ,     z ∈ U.
For every z ∈ U, use the rays
                                                 z + (λ − R0 )ω,        λ ⩾ R0 .
The separation and phase estimates of Proposition 8.4 hold uniformly on this family of rays. Since both
R0 and ω are fixed on U, all coefficients of the Volterra equation depend holomorphically on z, and its
contraction estimate is uniform. Hence the normalized fixed point depends holomorphically on z.


                                                              108
    We have so far solved the first-order system only in the radial direction. To check the remaining co-
ordinate directions, fix one coordinate and compare two ways of differentiating the constructed square-free
jet: differentiation with respect to the base point, and the corresponding coordinate equation of the full
square-free first-order system. Their difference satisfies the homogeneous radial system by flatness. The
estimates above hold uniformly for nearby base points, so this difference has zero normalization at infinity.
Raywise uniqueness forces it to vanish. Thus the constructed jet is a holomorphic horizontal section of the
                                                                     (U)
full flat system. Its empty component is a holomorphic solution Wϵ,η of the conjugated edge system, and
                           (U)
                               = 1 + O R−3/2 ,                (U)
                                                                  = O R−|I|−|A|−3/2 .
                                                                                  
                         Wϵ,η                       ∂tI ∂sA Wϵ,η

    The local solutions agree on overlaps. Indeed, all neighborhoods use the same vector ω. At a point
z ∈ U ∩ V, undo the radial scaling and write both constructions on the same geometric ray z + rω, r ⩾ 0.
They solve the same radial system and have the same normalization at infinity. Raywise uniqueness therefore
gives
                                          (U)    (V)
                                       Wϵ,η   = Wϵ,η     on U ∩ V.
The local solutions glue to a holomorphic function Wϵ,η on all of C. Setting

                                                Fϵ,η := Φϵ,η Wϵ,η

gives a holomorphic solution of the original edge system satisfying (6.43)–(6.44).
Step 6: Linear independence.
    For a square-free pair (I, A), the Airy asymptotics, the explicit logarithmic derivatives of Φϵ,η , and
(6.43)–(6.44) give, uniformly on C,
                                         !−1
                       Y√ Y β√                                   Y Y
                                             Φ−1                       ηa + O D−3/2 .
                                                                                     
                            ti        sa      ϵ,η ∂tI ∂sA Fϵ,η =  ϵi                                 (8.68)
                                  2
                       i∈I    a∈A                                   i∈I     a∈A

The leading matrix in (8.68), with rows indexed by (I, A) and columns indexed by (ϵ, η), is the character
table                                                 !
                                          Y Y
                                             ϵi    ηa
                                              i∈I   a∈A       (I,A),(ϵ,η)
        n+m
of {±1}     . It is invertible. Increasing D, if necessary, the exact normalized jet matrix is also invertible.
Therefore the 2n+m solutions are linearly independent.


A     Airy functions
We recall some standard facts about the Airy function Ai. For real x, it is given by the conditionally
convergent integral
                                               Z R      3     
                                        1               t
                                Ai(x) =    lim     cos     + xt dt.
                                        π R→∞ 0          3
The function Ai extends to an entire function on C, and it solves the Airy equation

                                             Ai′′ (w) − w Ai(w) = 0.                                     (A.1)

It is characterized by the decay condition

                                         Ai(w) → 0         as w → +∞

along the positive real axis. All zeros of Ai are real and negative; we write them as

                                             0 > a1 > a2 > a3 > · · · .

                                                      2/3
It is known that the Airy zeros satisfy ai ∼ − (3πi/2) . More precisely, for every i ∈ N, see for example [32,
eqs. (9.9.6) and (9.9.18)],
                                                      2/3
                                                   3πi
                                            ai +            ≲ i−1/3 .                                   (A.2)
                                                    2
We refer to [32, Chapter 9] for further background on Airy functions.

                                                          115
C     Proof of the edge local law
In this section, we prove Proposition 3.4. The proof is parallel to the bulk case. The main difference from
the bulk case is that the edge loop equation is centered around the algebraic relation s(w)2 = w, rather than
s(w)2 = −1. Thus the natural quantity to estimate is

                                                    P (w) := s(w)2 − w,
                                                                                                                      √
and one must use a stability estimate to convert moment bounds on P (w) into bounds on s(w) −                             w.

                                          X 1             
                                                         1
                                   s(w) =              −     −          .
                                               xi − w ai        Ai(0)
                                                       i⩾1

This proves (3.11).


D      Explicit solutions for β = 2 bulk system
We prove Theorem 6.1 in this section. The key idea is to remove the mixed interaction terms by factoring
out the mixed Vandermonde product
                                                                n m
                                                                Y Y
                                                   P (t, s) =           (ti − sa ).
                                                                i=1 a=1

After writing F = P G, the conjugated equations for G become the ordinary scalar An+m−1 -type system in
the combined variables
                                  x1 = t 1 , . . . , x n = t n , xn+a = sa .
Thus, when β = 2, the deformed system is gauge-equivalent, through multiplication by P (t, s), to the
standard scalar A-type CMS system (D.5)–(D.6); see [75].

Step 3. Linear independence. Choose v = (v1 , · · · , vn+m ) ∈ Cn+m such that

                                                             vr ̸= vu     (r ̸= u),

and
                                            Re[i σ · v] ̸= Re[i σ ′ · v]            for σ ̸= σ ′ .


                                                                       126
Such a choice is possible after excluding finitely many proper real hyperplanes.
   Along the ray x = Rv, as R → +∞, we have

                            Gσ (Rv) = cσ R−N+ (σ)N− (σ) exp(iR σ · v) 1 + O(R−1 ) ,
                                                                                 

where                           Y
                   cσ =                     (vr − vu )(σr σu −1)/2 ̸= 0,          N± (σ) := #{r : σr = ±1}.
                           1⩽r<u⩽n+m

The numbers Re[i σ · v] are pairwise distinct, so the exponentials exp(iR σ · v) have distinct growth rates
along this ray. Hence no nontrivial linear combination of the Gσ can vanish identically.
   Therefore the functions Gσ are linearly independent. Since Fσ = P Gσ and P is not identically zero, the
functions Fσ are also linearly independent. This proves the theorem.

                                                                   133
F       The equilibrium BBGKY hierarchy and the loop equations
In this appendix we explain that the equilibrium Bogoliubov–Born–Green–Kirkwood–Yvon equations for the
one-dimensional logarithmic interaction imply the loop equations (1.2), and therefore the particle system is
the Sineβ ensemble. This provides one example of a point process characterized by the BBGKY hierarchy
in the case of long-range and singular interactions. For the proof, we assume a priori mild estimates on the
point process, see (F.4).
    The definition and analysis of the BBGKY hierarchy for particle systems are delicate for long-range
interactions. We first discuss a general setting and then specialize to the log interaction in dimension 1.
Consider a simple point process               X
                                         µ=       δ x i , x i ∈ X = Rd ,
                                                         i∈I

and its correlation functions defined as the measures (ρp )p⩾1 such that
                                                        
                             X∗                              Z
                       E          f (xi1 , . . . , xip ) =   f (q1 , . . . , qp ) dρp (q1 , . . . , qp ),
                                i1 ,...,ip ∈I                       Xp

                                               P∗
for all suitable test functions f , where          means the summation is over all ordered p-tuples of distinct
indices. Assume µ is the local limit of a finite interacting particle system with the same charges and
symmetric, singular, long-range interaction potential φ (e.g. consider either Coulombic φ = (−∆)−1 or
logarithmic φ = − log below), at inverse temperature β: defining
                                             1 −βN α 21 Pi̸=j φ(yi −yj )−N PN

Depending on α ∈ R, the point process µ is expected to be either Poisson, crystallized, or a non-trivial

hierarchy (which corresponds to the BBGKY hierarchy at equilibrium) for the correlation functions of µ,
see [67, Equation (2.10)]: assuming some decay of correlations (called a clustering condition in [67]), we have

For singular φ, this pointwise identity (F.2) holds only away from the collision diagonals. It does not by
itself imply the following weak formulation, as this would require a priori information on the behavior of ρp
near collisions. We therefore adopt the following distributional BBGKY hierarchy as the definition.
Definition F.1 (Equilibrium BBGKY). We say that µ satisfies the equilibrium BBGKY hierarchy for the
interaction φ if for any p ⩾ 1 and any test function Φ ∈ Cc∞ (X p ), we have
               X 1                                                        Z                           
           E             ∇xi1 Φ(xi1 , . . . , xip ) − Φ(xi1 , . . . , xip ) ∇φ(xi1 − u)(dµi1 − dρ1 )(u) = 0, (F.3)
                       β
               i1 ,...,ip ∈I

where the indices i1 , . . . , ip are not required to be distinct and we denote µi = µ − δxi .

Remark F.2. For symmetric interactions, constant density of states ρ1 , and ∇φ ∈ L1 we have ∇φ(q −
                                                                                                   R

   Finally, we explain how the loop equations (1.2) allow us to obtain the equilibrium hierarchy (F.2). The
